Q1   Answer briefly:   [20]
Define biology and explain why it is considered both an independent and an
interdisciplinary science.
Definition - 1 marks
Independent science explanation 1.5 Marks
Interdisciplinary science explanation - 1.5 Marks
Biology is the study of living organisms and their interactions with each other
and the environment. It is a natural science that seeks to understand the
a. processes that give rise to life, sustain it, and allow it to evolve and diversify.   4
CO-1; BL-1
Biology is an independent scientific discipline because it has its own methods,
principles, and theories that distinguish it from other sciences. These include
observation, experimentation, hypothesis testing, and peer review.
Biology is also an interdisciplinary science that draws on knowledge and
techniques from other fields, such as chemistry, physics, mathematics, and
computer science. This allows biologists to tackle complex problems and
make significant advances in areas such as genetics, evolution, ecology, and
biotechnology.
Describe the law of dominance and differentiate between complete
dominance and incomplete dominance using suitable examples.
Law of Dominance – 1 Mark
Complete dominance – 1.5 marks
CO-3; BL-2   b. Incomplete dominance – 1.5 Marks   4
The law of dominance explains that when two alleles are present in an
organism, one allele (dominant) expresses itself and masks the effect of the
other allele (recessive).
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
In complete dominance, the dominant allele completely masks the expression
of the recessive allele in a heterozygous individual, and only the dominant
phenotype is observed.
Example: In pea plants, the allele for purple flower color (P) is dominant over
white (p), so heterozygous plants (Pp) show purple flowers.
In incomplete dominance, neither allele is completely dominant, and both
alleles blend to produce an intermediate phenotype. Example: In snapdragons,
a red-flowered plant (RR) crossed with a white-flowered plant (WW)
produces pink flowers (RW).
Illustrate the functions of oxidoreductases and transferases, including the
types of reactions they catalyze with one example each. Also discuss
importance of enzyme specificity.
oxidoreductases & transferases – 2 Marks
Enzyme specificity – 2 Marks
Oxidoreductases enzymes are involved in oxidation-reduction reactions,
where electrons are transferred between molecules. Examples include
dehydrogenases (e.g., alcohol dehydrogenase) that remove hydrogen atoms
CO-4; BL-2   c. from substrates and transfer them to coenzymes like NAD+   4
Transferases facilitate the transfer of functional groups (e.g., methyl,
phosphate, amino) from one molecule to another. Kinases, which transfer
phosphate groups, are notable examples.
importance of enzyme specificity
Specificity ensures enzymes bind only to their correct substrates. It arises
from hydrogen bonding and the shape of the active site. This prevents the
enzyme from acting on unrelated molecules, maintaining reaction
accuracy.
State endergonic and exergonic reactions with one example of each, and
mention their role in maintaining energy balance in living systems.
endergonic reactions 1.5 Marks
exergonic reactions   1.5 Marks
energy balance   1 Mark
Endergonic reactions are chemical reactions that require an input of energy to
proceed. In these reactions, the energy of the products is higher than that of the
reactants. This means that the reactants absorb energy from their surroundings
to form products with higher energy content. Endergonic reactions are often
associated with energy-absorbing processes, such as photosynthesis, where
plants capture and store energy from sunlight to convert carbon dioxide and
CO-5; BL-1   d. water into glucose.   4
Exergonic reactions, on the other hand, release energy as they proceed. In these
reactions, the energy of the products is lower than that of the reactants. This
means that excess energy is liberated and can be used to perform cellular work.
Exergonic reactions are characteristic of energy-releasing processes, such as
cellular respiration, where the breakdown of glucose results in the release of
energy that is captured in the form of ATP (Adenosine Triphosphate).
Together, endergonic and exergonic reactions play a crucial role in
maintaining the overall energy balance within living systems. Endergonic
reactions store energy, while exergonic reactions release it. This balance
ensures that cells have access to the energy they need for various
physiological functions, maintaining the vitality and stability of biological
systems.
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
Discuss the significance of the four phases of bacterial growth in ensuring
population stability and survival.
Lag Phase -1 Mark
Log Phase - 1 Mark
Stationary Phase 1 Mark
Death Phase   - 1 Mark
CO-5; BL-2   e. Lag Phase Helps bacteria adapt and acclimatize to new environmental conditions   4
before active growth begins.
Log Phase Enables rapid multiplication and efficient colonization when nutrients
are abundant.
Stationary Phase Maintains balance between growth and death, preventing
depletion of resources and ensuring population stability.
Death Phase Reduces population size to avoid overpopulation, while some cells
survive, contributing to resilience of the population.
Demonstrate the transmission of genetic disorders by carrier parents through
autosomal recessive and X-linked inheritance. Also highlight the differences
in inheritance risk between autosomal recessive and X-linked traits
.Explanation of autosomal recessive inheritance & X-linked inheritance– 4
marks
Differences in inheritance risk – 1 Mark
Autosomal Recessive: Both copies of the gene must be mutated to manifest
the disorder (e.g., cystic fibrosis). Recessive diseases are single-gene
disorders that only occur in the homozygous state - when an individual
carries two mutant versions (alleles) of the relevant gene. The effects of the
healthy allele can compensate for the effects of the mutant allele. The mutant
allele does not cause disease symptoms when a healthy allele is also present.
Recessive diseases are more difficult to trace through family trees because
carriers of a mutant allele do not show symptoms of the disease.   It,
therefore, appears that the disease has skipped a generation when it is seen
in groups of children within a family. The risk of an individual having a
Q2   recessive disorder increases when two people who are closely related have
a.   [05]
CO-3; BL-3   a child together (consanguinity). This is because there is a much greater
chance that the same mutant allele will be present in related parents.
Example: Cystic Fibrosis, Sickle Cell Anaemia   However, if a parent
inherits two mutant alleles, there are no healthy alleles, so the mutant allele
can exert its effect.
X-linked: The mutated gene is located on the X chromosome, and the
disorder predominantly affects males (e.g., haemophilia A, which is an X-
linked recessive disorder). X-linked disorders are single-gene disorders
that result from the presence of a mutated gene on the X chromosome.
Because females (XX) have two copies of the X chromosome, but males
(XY) only have one copy, X-linked disorders are more common in males.
If a male’s single copy on the X chromosome is mutated, he has no
healthy copy to restore healthy function. The inheritance patterns of X-
linked diseases are simplified by the fact that males always pass their X
chromosome to their daughters but never to their sons. Like other single-
gene disorders, X-linked disorders can be either recessive or dominant.
Examples: Rett syndrome (a brain disorder)
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
Differences / Key Points in inheritance risk : Autosomal recessive
disorders affect both sexes equally and may skip generations. X-linked
disorders predominantly affect males, while females are usually carriers.
Carrier status determines the risk of affected children,
Define the three types of biomimicry. Give one suitable example for each
with a brief explanation. Also state the advantages of using biomimicry in
the design of the Shinkansen Series 500 train.
three types of biomimicry with examples- 3X3 = 9 Marks
advantages of using biomimicry – 1 mark
Biomimicry of Designs: This type of biomimicry involves imitating the
form and structure of natural designs, such as the shape of a bird's wing or
the structure of a shell. By mimicking these designs, engineers and
designers can develop new products and technologies that are more
efficient, sustainable, and resilient.
Example: Velcro is a classic example of biomimicry of designs.
The inventor, George de Mestral, was inspired by the way burrs stuck to
his dog's fur during a walk in the Swiss Alps. He examined the burrs under
a microscope and found that they had tiny hooks that caught on the loops
of his dog's fur. He then developed a synthetic version of this
design, with tiny hooks on one side and loops on the other, creating the
now-famous "hook-and-loop" fastener known as Velcro.
Biomimicry of Processes: This type of biomimicry involves imitating
natural processes and systems, such as photosynthesis or the water cycle.
By mimicking these processes, engineers and designers can develop new
technologies and systems that are more sustainable, efficient,
and resilient.
Q2   b. Example: The Eastgate Centre in Harare, Zimbabwe, is a building that   [10]
CO-1; BL-1   uses biomimicry of processes to regulate its temperature. The building was
designed to mimic the ventilation system of termite mounds, which use a
system of vents and tunnels to regulate their temperature. The Eastgate
Centre uses a similar system, with vents that open and close to regulate the
flow of air, reducing the need for air conditioning and heating.
Biomimicry of Ecosystems: This type of biomimicry involves imitating
the way natural ecosystems function, such as the way nutrients are cycled
through a forest or the way a coral reef supports a diverse community of
organisms. By mimicking these ecosystems, engineers and designers can
develop new technologies and systems that are more sustainable, resilient,
and supportive of biodiversity.
Example: The city of Portland, Oregon, is working to develop a
stormwater management system that mimics the way a forest ecosystem
function. The system, known as "ecoroofs," involves planting vegetation
on rooftops to absorb and filter rainwater, reducing the amount of
stormwater runoff and improving the quality of water that enters the city's
rivers and streams. The eco-roofs also provide habitat for birds and insects,
increasing biodiversity in the city.
Advantages of using biomimicry: High-speed travel due to reduced drag
(kingfisher beak design). Lower noise and vibration (owl wings and
penguin feet inspiration). Less energy required due to reduced drag and
vibration. Reduced wear and tear on tracks; lower maintenance cost
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Describe how enzymes affect activation energy and explain the role of
enzyme-substrate interaction in speeding up chemical reactions.
Enzymes and activation energy + diagram – 2 +1 marks
Role of enzyme-substrate interaction – 2 Marks
In any chemical reaction, reactant molecules need to acquire a certain amount
of energy to kick-start the process. This initial energy requirement is called
activation energy. Enzymes are like catalysts, which means they speed up
chemical reactions without being consumed in the process. Enzymes have
specific binding sites where reactant molecules, called substrates, attach
themselves. This interaction is highly specific. When substrates bind to an
enzyme's active site, the enzyme changes the substrates' shape, making it easier
for them to react. It's as if the enzyme carves a shorter, less steep path over the
energy hill. This reduces the activation energy required for the reaction to
proceed. With the lower activation energy, the reaction can happen much faster
and more efficiently than it would without the enzyme. Once the reaction is
complete, the enzyme releases the product, which is the result of the chemical
transformation. Importantly, the enzyme remains unchanged and ready to assist
in another reaction.
Q2   c.   [05]
CO-4; BL-2
Enzymes act as biological catalysts that speed up chemical reactions
without being used up. They lower the activation energy required for a
reaction to begin, making it easier for the reaction to proceed. Enzymes
have specific binding sites called active sites where substrates bind. This
enzyme–substrate interaction is highly specific, like a lock and key fit.
When the substrate binds to the active site, the enzyme changes the shape
of the substrate, making it easier for the reaction to occur. This reduces the
activation energy barrier. As a result, the reaction occurs faster and more
efficiently, leading to quicker product formation. After the reaction, the
enzyme releases the product and remains unchanged for reuse.
Describe the main types of lipids with suitable examples and discuss its
structure and function. Also explain the role of lipids in living cells.
Main types of lipids & structure, function, and examples of each type – 2X4=8
Marks
Role of lipids in living cells -2 Marks
Q3
CO-2; BL-2   a. Triglycerides are a type of simple lipid that are composed of a glycerol   [10]
molecule and three fatty acids. They are the most common type of lipid found
in the body and are primarily used as a source of energy. Triglycerides can be
classified into two main types based on the type of fatty acids they contain:
saturated and unsaturated. Saturated triglycerides have only saturated fatty
acids, which are solid at room temperature and are typically found in animal
products such as meat and dairy. Unsaturated triglycerides have one or more
unsaturated fatty acids, which are liquid at room temperature and are typically
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
found in plant-based oils such as olive oil and sunflower oil. High Triglyceride
levels in the blood are often measured as part of a lipid profile to assess the risk
of cardiovascular disease.
Phospholipids are a type of complex lipid and a major component of cell
membranes. They have a unique structure with both hydrophobic and
hydrophilic regions. Phospholipids are found in various biological membranes
and play a critical role in maintaining membrane structure and function.
Sphingolipids are another class of complex lipids that are important
components of cell membranes, particularly in nerve tissue. They are composed
of a sphingosine backbone, a fatty acid chain, and a polar head group, and are
involved in several cellular processes such as cell signalling and membrane
stability
Steroids: Steroids are a class of complex lipids that play important roles in
the body, including regulating metabolism, immune function, and
reproductive processes. They have a unique structure consisting of four fused
rings and vary in the functional groups attached to the rings. Examples of
steroids include cholesterol, which is important for cell membrane structure
and function, and hormone steroids such as testosterone and estrogen, which
play roles in sexual development and reproductive function Steroids are
synthesized from cholesterol and other precursors in various tissues, including
the adrenal glands, ovaries, and testes.
Wax: Waxes are a type of lipid that serve as protective coatings found on the
surface of plants and animals. They are composed of long-chain fatty acids
and alcohols, and often have a high melting point due to their highly saturated
structure. Examples of waxes include beeswax, which is produced by
honeybees and used to make honeycomb, and carnauba wax, which is derived
from the leaves of a Brazilian palm tree and used in various applications such
as cosmetics, food, and automotive products. In animals, waxes serve a variety
of functions such as waterproofing feathers, fur, and skin, and protecting
against predators and parasites. In plants, waxes help to prevent water loss and
protect against UV radiation, pathogens, and herbivores.
Role of lipids in living cells- Lipids form a major part of cell membranes,
helping regulate the entry and exit of molecules - They support hormone
production and vitamin absorption - Lipids act as a source of energy storage and
movement in the body
Explain the process of DNA replication by describing the roles of key
enzymes involved, and include an explanation of why DNA replication is
called semi-conservative.
DNA Replication – 2 marks
Roles of key enzymes – 6 Marks
Semi-conservative Nature – 2 Marks
Q3   b. DNA replication, or semi-conservative replication, is the process by which DNA   [10]
CO-3; BL-2   is essentially doubled. It is an important process that takes place within the
dividing cell. DNA replication is a complex process that ensures the accurate
duplication of genetic material. It involves the coordination of various enzymes to
unwind the DNA double helix, synthesize new strands, and proofread for errors.
Helicase is the initial enzyme in DNA replication. Its primary function is to
unwind the DNA double helix. It accomplishes this by breaking the hydrogen
bonds between the complementary base pairs, such as A-T and G-C. By doing
so, helicase separates the two DNA strands, creating what is called a
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
"replication fork." This single-stranded DNA region is where the actual
replication process will take place.
Primase follows helicase. Its role is to synthesize RNA primers. Primase adds
short RNA sequences, known as primers, to the DNA template strands. These
primers are essential because DNA Polymerase, the enzyme responsible for
adding new DNA nucleotides during replication, can only extend an existing
strand. On the leading strand, primase synthesizes a single RNA primer at the
5' end, providing a starting point for DNA Polymerase.
DNA Polymerase III is the primary enzyme responsible for DNA synthesis
during replication. It adds nucleotides to the growing DNA strand. On the
leading strand, DNA Polymerase III synthesizes the new DNA strand in a
continuous manner by extending from the 5' to 3' direction, using the parental
DNA strand as a template. This strand doesn't encounter the same challenges
as the lagging strand, which requires a more intricate process.
Exonucleases come into play after DNA Polymerase III. Their function is to
remove RNA primers from the DNA template. Once the RNA primers are
synthesized by primase, exonucleases degrade them, creating gaps in the DNA
strands. On the lagging strand, multiple RNA primers are synthesized at
different points along the template to provide starting points for DNA
Polymerase.
DNA Polymerase I is an enzyme responsible for gap-filling. After the
removal of RNA primers by exonucleases, DNA Polymerase I replaces the
RNA nucleotides with DNA nucleotides. On the lagging strand, DNA
Polymerase I works in a discontinuous manner, filling in the gaps created by
the removal of RNA primer.
Ligase is the final enzyme involved in DNA replication. Its function is to seal
the nicks or gaps in the DNA backbone. It does so by catalyzing the formation
of phosphodiester bonds between adjacent nucleotides. On the lagging strand,
ligase joins the Okazaki fragments together, ensuring the integrity and
continuity of the newly synthesized DNA strand.
Semi-conservative Nature of Replication :DNA replication is called semi-
conservative because each new DNA molecule contains, One original
(parental) strand One newly synthesized strandThis ensures accurate transfer
of genetic information to daughter cells.
Compare fungi, protozoa, archaea, algae, and viruses with respect to their
characteristics, cellular structure, and practical significance.
Fungi, Protozoa, Archaea, Algae, and Viruses -2X5 – 10 Marks
Fungi: Cellular Structure & Characteristics: Eukaryotic, mostly multicellular
(except yeast), chitin cell walls, heterotrophic, reproduce sexually and asexually.
Q4   Practical Significance: Decomposers in ecosystems; used in food production
CO-5; BL-2   a.   (bread, cheese); some pathogenic (e.g., Candida). (1 mark)   [10]
Protozoa : Cellular Structure & Characteristics: Eukaryotic, unicellular,
heterotrophic, free-living or parasitic, complex cell structures (cilia, flagella).
Practical Significance: Play roles in food chains; some pathogenic (e.g.,
Plasmodium causing malaria); indicator organisms for water quality. (1 mark)
Archaea : Cellular Structure & Characteristics: Prokaryotic, unicellular, distinct
biochemistry, often extremophiles, reproduce asexually by binary fission.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
Practical Significance: Decomposers; important in biotechnology and genetic
research; help understand early Earth conditions.
Algae : Cellular Structure & Characteristics: Eukaryotic, mostly aquatic,
photosynthetic, diverse forms (green, brown, red algae), cell walls made of
cellulose. Practical Significance: Major oxygen producers; base of aquatic food
chains; potential sources of biofuels.
Viruses : Cellular Structure & Characteristics: Acellular, contain DNA or RNA
enclosed in protein coat, obligate intracellular parasites, replicate inside host cells
. Practical Significance: Cause diseases (e.g., HIV, Influenza); important in
biotechnology (gene therapy); advance molecular biology studies.

CO2 (released per cycle)

cycle must turn twice per glucose molecule.
Final Products (per glucose molecule):
2 ATP (or GTP)
6 NADH
2 FADH2

Q5   Discuss the application of enzymes in different industries such as   [10]
b.
CO-4; BL-2   pharmaceuticals, food processing, and detergents by describing the role of
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
specific enzymes in each. Also, state the significance of enzymes in industrial
processes.
Pharmaceuticals -2 Marks
Food processing - 2 Marks
Detergent industry -2 Marks
Biofuels industry -1 Mark
Paper & pulp industry -1 Mark
Significance of enzymes – 2 Marks
1. Pharmaceuticals: Enzymes: Nitrile hydratase, transaminase,
monoamine oxidase, lipase, penicillin acylase Functions:
Synthesize intermediates for active pharmaceutical ingredients,
enhancing drug production efficiency.
2. Food Processing: Enzymes: Trypsin, amylase, glucose isomerase,
papain, pectinase Functions: Convert starch to glucose, clarify
fruit juices, improve bread texture, and accelerate cheese
production.
3. Detergent Industry: Detergent Industry: Enzymes: Protease,
lipase, amylase, cellulase Functions: Break down protein, fats,
starches, and cellulose in stains for effective cleaning.
4. Biofuels: Enzymes: Lipase, cellulase, xylanase Functions:
Decompose lignocellulosic materials to produce bioethanol and
fatty acid methyl esters.
5. Paper and Pulp: Enzymes: Lipase, cellulase, xylanase Functions:
Remove lignin for improved bleaching and fiber quality, reducing
chemical use.
Significance of Enzymes in Industrial Applications: Enzymes speed up
chemical reactions without harsh conditions, reducing energy use and
harmful chemicals. This improves the sustainability, efficiency, and cost-
effectiveness of many industrial processes.
Describe how Genetically Modified Organisms are used in agriculture to
enhance crop quality and yield. Use Golden Rice and Bt Cotton as
examples to describe the traits introduced, their mode of action, benefits,
and their impact on society and the environment.
Role of GMOs in agriculture – 2 marks
Golden Rice – 3 marks
Bt Cotton – 3 marks
Impact on society/environment – 2 marks
Q6
CO-1;BL-2   a. Genetically Modified Organisms (GMOs) are developed using agricultural   [10]
biotechnology to introduce desirable traits into crops, such as enhanced
nutrition, pest resistance, or stress tolerance. This improves crop
productivity, quality, and sustainability.
Golden Rice Genetically modified (transgenic) rice. Trait Introduced:
beta-carotene (pro-vitamin A) in the rice endosperm. Mode of Action: The
inserted genes (from daffodil or maize) enable rice to synthesize beta-
carotene, which the human body converts into Vitamin A after
consumption. Purpose & Significance: Prevents Vitamin A deficiency,
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
reducing risks of blindness and improving nutrition in populations relying
on rice.
Bt Cotton Genetically modified (transgenic) cotton. Trait Introduced:
Contains Cry toxin gene from Bacillus thuringiensis. Mode of Action: When
insect pests (like bollworms) feed on the cotton, the Cry protein binds to
their gut cells, forming pores that disrupt ion balance and cause insect death.
Purpose & Significance: Provides insect resistance, reduces pesticide use,
increases yield, and lowers farming costs
Impact on Society and Environment: Enhances food security and public
health through nutritious crops. Supports sustainable agriculture by
reducing pesticide use and conserving resources
In the context of cancer immunotherapy, explain how a nanoparticle-based
vaccine delivery system is developed with respect to the selection of suitable
nanoparticles, the encapsulation of tumor antigens and adjuvants, the strategy
used for targeting immune cells, and the therapeutic outcomes obtained.
Selection of nanoparticles -2 Marks
Encapsulation of tumour antigens and adjuvants – 3 Marks
Targeting of immune cells – 2 Marks
Therapeutic outcomes – 3 Marks
A nanoparticle-based vaccine delivery system for cancer immunotherapy is
designed to improve the delivery of tumor antigens and enhance the
immune response against cancer cells.
1. Selection of nanoparticles:
Q6   Biodegradable polymeric nanoparticles such as PLGA (poly(lactic-co-glycolic
b.   acid)) are used because they are safe, biocompatible, and effective in   [10]
CO-5; BL-2   encapsulating therapeutic molecules.
2. Encapsulation of tumor antigens and adjuvants:
Tumor-associated antigens (TAAs) and immune-stimulating adjuvants such as
CpG oligonucleotides are encapsulated within the nanoparticles. This protects
them from degradation in the body and allows for controlled and sustained
release.
3. Targeting of immune cells:
The surface of nanoparticles is modified, for example with mannose, to
specifically target dendritic cells, which are important antigen-presenting cells.
This enhances uptake of nanoparticles and improves antigen presentation to the
immune system.
4. Therapeutic outcomes:
This system leads to stronger activation of cytotoxic T cells, which helps in
destroying cancer cells. It also reduces systemic toxicity due to targeted delivery,
slows tumor growth, and improves survival rates in experimental studies. It also
offers potential for personalized cancer treatment using patient-specific tumor
antigens.
Q7   a. List the roles of biology in climate change, food security, disease control,   [05]
CO-1; BL-1   environmental protection, and sustainable development.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
Climate Change – 1 Mark
Food Security   - 1 Mark
Diseases control 1 Mark
Environmental protection - 1 Mark
Sustainable development - 1 Mark
Role in Climate Change :Biology helps in understanding ecosystems and
how organisms respond to environmental changes. This knowledge aids in
developing strategies like conservation and carbon management.
Role in Food Security: Biology improves agricultural practices by
enhancing crop yield, pest resistance, and nutritional value through
techniques like genetic modification.
Role in Disease Control :Biology helps identify causes of diseases and
supports the development of vaccines, medicines, and preventive measures
to control their spread.
Environmental Protection :Biological studies help in conserving
biodiversity, protecting endangered species, and maintaining ecological
balance.
Sustainable Development Biology contributes to developing eco-friendly
technologies and sustainable practices that reduce environmental impact
Explain the following with suitable examples;
i.   Enzymes
ii.   Hormones.
iii.   Contractile Proteins
iv.   Structural polysaccharides
v.   Disaccharides
Enzymes   - 2 Marks
Hormones   - 2 Marks
Contractile Proteins - 2 Marks
structural Polysaccharides - 2 Marks
Disaccharides 2 Marks
Q7   Enzymes: Many proteins function as enzymes to catalyze biochemical
b. reactions that occur in the living world. Enzymes play a key role in   [10]
CO-2; BL-2   metabolism. Every enzyme is specific in action, e.g., amylase, protease,
RuBisCO (Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate carboxylase/oxygenase), which is the
most abundant enzyme found in plants and the principal enzyme involved in
photosynthesis.
Hormones: Some hormones are proteinaceous, e.g., insulin (sugar
metabolism-regulating hormone) and parathyroid hormone (calcium and
phosphate transport-regulating hormone). Hormones play a key role in the
regulation of metabolism.
Contractile Proteins: Myosin and actin make the muscle fibres contractile to
bring about movements and locomotion. The contractile system is basically
made up of the protein actin, but association with myosin is essential for
contraction.
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
structural polysaccharides. Structural polysaccharides provide support and
rigidity to cells and tissues, while storage polysaccharides are used to store
energy. Examples of structural polysaccharides include cellulose, chitin, and
peptidoglycan. Cellulose is found in plant cell walls, chitin is found in the
exoskeletons of insects and crustaceans, and peptidoglycan is found in
bacterial cell walls.
Disaccharides are a type of carbohydrate that are composed of two
monosaccharide units linked together by a glycosidic bond. Upon hydrolysis,
the glycosidic bond is broken, and the disaccharide is split into two separate
monosaccharides. The two monosaccharides that make up a disaccharide can
be either the same or different. One of the most well-known disaccharides is
sucrose, which is composed of glucose and fructose units linked together.
When sucrose is hydrolyzed, it breaks down into its constituent
monosaccharides of glucose and fructose. Two other important disaccharides
are maltose and lactose. Maltose is composed of two glucose units linked
together, while lactose is composed of a glucose unit and a galactose unit
linked together
Classify the living organisms based on nitrogenous excretion with respect
to their nitrogenous waste products, toxicity, water requirement, and
suitable examples.
Classification – 0.5 Mark
Ammonotelic 1.5 Marks
Ureotelic   1.5 Marks
Uricotelic   1.5 Marks
Organisms are classified based on their nitrogenous waste products into
ammonotelic, ureotelic, and uricotelic types, depending on toxicity and water
requirement.
Ammonotelic : Organisms that primarily excrete ammonia (NH3) as their
primary nitrogenous waste product. Ammonia is highly soluble in water and
toxic to tissues. It is a very toxic substance to tissues and extremely soluble in
water. Only one nitrogen atom is removed with it. A lot of water is needed for
the excretion of ammonia to maintain ammonia levels in the excretory fluid
Q7   c. below the level in body fluids to prevent toxicity. Ammonotelic animals, like   [05]
CO-5; BL-2   aquatic protozoans, crustaceans, and echinoderms, excrete ammonia directly
into the water. Examples: Fish: Many aquatic fish excrete excess ammonia into
the water as it is highly soluble and can be eliminated through diffusion.
Amphibians: Amphibians, like frogs, often excrete ammonia, which is excreted
through their moist skin and kidneys.
Ureotellic: Organisms that excrete urea (a less toxic nitrogenous compound) as
their primary nitrogenous waste product. Urea is less toxic and requires less
water for excretion.. Ureotelic animals, such as humans and amphibians,
efficiently excrete urea in less water. Examples Mammals: Most mammals,
including humans, excrete urea through the kidneys as it requires less water for
excretion compared to ammonia. Marine Birds: Birds excrete urea in a semi-
solid form as a way to conserve water in their saltwater environments. 3.
Uricotellic: Organisms that excrete uric acid, a highly insoluble nitrogenous
compound, as their primary nitrogenous waste product. Uric acid is highly
insoluble in water, requiring very little water for excretion. Uric acid is less
toxic than ammonia or urea. It can be stored in body tissues without toxic
effects and is the most efficient nitrogenous waste removal method. Uricotelic
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
animals typically have white pasty excreta, and some mammals, including
humans, excrete uric acid as a component of their urine in small amounts.
Examples: Reptiles (excluding birds): Many reptiles, such as snakes and
lizards, excrete uric acid, allowing them to conserve water in arid
environments. Birds: While birds are ureotellic, they further convert urea into
uric acid to minimize water loss, particularly in their concentrated urine and
solid excreta.
********** 14 **********
````

1. Yeast   [10]
2. Archaea
3. Bacteria
4. Algae
5. Virus
-   Any two characteristics, explanation, and at least one example (1M+0.5M+0.5M) ×5

Q5   a. In humans, free earlobes are determined by a dominant allele (E), while
attached earlobes are determined by a recessive allele (e). Given one parent
with genotype EE and another with genotype ee, determine the genotypic and   [10]
phenotypic ratios of the offspring across the F₁ and F₂ generations?
F₁ Results:
(5M)
Genotype: 100% Ee (heterozygous)
Phenotype: 100% free earlobes
Ratio: 1 (free earlobes)

earlobes (ee)

2) Bacteria,   [10]

Explain the role of ribosomes, transfer RNA and messenger RNA in
protein synthesis.
the role of ribosomes, - 1 .5 Marks
transfer RNA (tRNA) – 1 Mark
messenger RNA (mRNA) in protein synthesis – 1.5 Marks
Role of ribosomes:
Ribosomes initiate the translation process. They consist of a larger subunit
and a smaller subunit. The smaller subunit binds the mRNA, while the larger   4
CO-2; SO-2; BL-2   b.
subunit holds two tRNA molecules close together so that a peptide bond can
be formed using energy.
Role of transfer RNA (tRNA):
tRNA brings amino acids to the ribosome and has anticodons complementary
to mRNA codons. Two tRNA molecules bind to adjacent codons and are
positioned close together in the ribosome, allowing peptide bond formation
between their amino acids.
Role of messenger RNA (mRNA)
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
mRNA carries the genetic code in the form of codons. It enters the smaller
ribosomal subunit and is held by tRNA molecules with complementary
codons. It acts as a template for arranging amino acids in the correct sequence.
Describe the terms Vmax and KM in enzyme kinetics. Illustrate their
implications using a Michaelis-Menten graph.
Vmax (1.5 marks)
KM (1.5 marks)
Graph (1 Mark)
Vmax - the Maximum Speed- enzymes have a maximum speed referred to as
Vmax. It signifies the highest rate at which an enzyme can catalyze a reaction
under specific conditions. Vmax shows the maximum reaction rate an enzyme
can achieve; it is the plateau of the curve where increasing substrate
concentration no longer increases the rate.
KM - the Affinity: A high KM value implies that the enzyme needs a
substantial substrate concentration to achieve half of its maximum speed
(Vmax). Conversely, a low KM indicates that the enzyme is highly selective,
CO-3 ; SO-2; BL-2   c. requiring only a minimal substrate amount to reach that point./ Using the   4
graph, KM shows the substrate concentration at which the reaction rate is half
of Vmax, reflecting the enzyme’s affinity for its substrate.

Using Huntington’s disease as an example, show that autosomal dominant
inheritance is caused by a single-gene mutation. Demonstrate the
inheritance pattern and interpret how the disease is passed from parents
to offspring
Autosomal dominant inheritance – 1 Marks
Demonstration with Huntington’s disease -1 Marks
parents to offspring with diagram- 2 Marks
Inheritance pattern – 1 Mark
Autosomal dominant inheritance is caused by a single-gene mutation because
only one copy of an altered gene (mutation) on a non-sex chromosome is
necessary to cause the disease- Inheritance of a single mutated copy of the
gene from either parent is sufficient to cause the disorder (e.g., Huntington's
disease). Dominant diseases are single gene disorders that occur in the
heterozygous state – when an individual has one mutant copy of the relevant
gene and one healthy copy. The effects of the mutant version of the gene
(allele) override the effects of the healthy version of the gene. So, the mutant
allele causes disease symptoms even though a healthy allele is present.
Dominant disorders tend to crop up in every generation of an affected family
because everyone carrying a dominant mutant allele shows the symptoms of
the disease. Dominant disorders spread vertically down family trees, from
Q2   a. parent to child. In rare cases, when an individual has two copies of the mutant   [05]
CO-2; SO-2; BL-3   gene (also known as being homozygous), the disorder symptoms are generally
more severe.

Explain how biotechnology is used to develop BT cotton. Describe its
mechanism of action, list its advantages, and discuss its potential concerns
associated with its cultivation
BT cotton (2 Marks)
Mechanism of Action with explanation (4 marks)
Advantages (2 marks)
Potential Concerns: (2 marks)
BT cotton (
Bt cotton is a genetically modified crop created using biotechnology by
inserting the Bt gene from the bacterium Bacillus thuringiensis into cotton
plants. This enables the plant to resist specific insect pests, especially the
cotton bollworm.Mechanism of Action with explanation - The Bt gene
produces Cry proteins - insecticidal toxins- in cotton tissues Genetic
Modification: Specific genes (cry) from Bacillus thuringiensis are inserted
into the cotton plant’s DNA.Toxin Production: The engineered cotton plant
produces inactive protoxins (Cry proteins) in its tissues, particularly in leaves
and bolls.Ingestion by Pests: Caterpillar pests, such as the cotton bollworm
or tobacco budworm, feed on the plant and ingest the protoxin.Activation in
the Gut: The alkaline pH levels in the insect's midgut dissolve the crystal
Q2   b.   proteins, activating them into potent toxins.Receptor Binding and Cell   [10]
CO-1; SO-1; BL-1
Death: The activated toxin binds to cadherin-like receptors on the gut
epithelium. This destroys the gut membrane lining, creating pores that lead to
cell swelling, lysis, and eventually the death of the insect due to infection or
starvation
Advantages
Pest resistance: Protects the plant from major insect pests.
Reduced pesticide use: Safer for farmers and the environment.
Higher crop yield: Less damage from pests increases productivity.
Economic benefits: Lowers expenditure on pesticides and increases farmers’
income.
Potential Concerns:
Resistance development: Continuous exposure may cause pests to become
resistant to Bt toxin.
Non-target effects: Possible impact on beneficial insects and local ecosystems.
Biodiversity: Widespread cultivation of GM crops may reduce genetic
diversity.
Regulatory and ethical issues: Concerns about GM crop safety and long-term
effects.
List the different classes of enzymes based on the type of reactions they
catalyze and cite an example of each
List of different class of enzymes – 1 Mark
Enzymes with Explanation & Examples – 4 Marks
Q2   Oxidoreductases : These enzymes are involved in oxidation-reduction   [05]
c.
CO-3; SO-2; BL-1   reactions, where electrons are transferred between molecules. Examples
include dehydrogenases (e.g., alcohol dehydrogenase) that remove hydrogen
atoms from substrates and transfer them to coenzymes like NAD+
Transferases : Transferases facilitate the transfer of functional groups (e.g.,
methyl, phosphate, amino) from one molecule to another. Kinases, which
transfer phosphate groups, are notable examples.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Hydrolases : Hydrolases catalyze hydrolysis reactions, breaking down
molecules by adding water. Lipases (break down lipids), proteases (digest
proteins), and amylases (hydrolyze starches) are common hydrolases. 4.
Lyases : Lyases promote the elimination or addition of groups to substrates,
often resulting in the formation of double bonds or the breaking of existing
bonds. Examples include decarboxylases (remove carboxyl groups) and
dehydratases.
Isomerases rearrange molecular structures by transferring functional groups
within a molecule. An example is glucose-6-phosphate isomerase, which
converts glucose-6 phosphate to fructose-6-phosphate. 6. Ligases (EC 6):
Ligases join two molecules, often using energy from ATP, to create a new,
larger molecule. DNA ligase, involved in DNA replication and repair, is a
well-known ligase.
Describe simple and complex carbohydrates. Also Classify
polysaccharides and provide examples for each type
Simple and complex carbohydrates – (5 marks)
Simple carbohydrates are a type of carbohydrate that consist of one or two
sugar molecules, also known as monosaccharides and disaccharides,
respectively.
Monosaccharides are the smallest type of simple carbohydrate, as they
contain just one sugar unit. Monosaccharides are a type of carbohydrate that
cannot be broken down into simpler units of polyhydroxy aldehyde or ketone.
They are the simplest form of carbohydrate and are sometimes referred to as
simple sugars.
Disaccharides are a type of carbohydrate that are composed of two
monosaccharide units linked together by a glycosidic bond. Upon hydrolysis,
the glycosidic bond is broken, and the disaccharide is split into two separate
monosaccharides.
Q3
a. Complex carbohydrates, also known as polysaccharides, are a type of   [10]
CO-3; SO-2; BL-2
carbohydrate that consist of many sugar molecules linked together in long
chains.
Polysaccharides classification (polysaccharides; homo- and
heteropolysaccharides with suitable examples) – (5 marks)
Polysaccharides are a type of complex carbohydrate that consist of many
sugar molecules linked together in long chains. They can be classified based
on their composition and their function.
Based on composition, polysaccharides can be classified into two main
categories: homo- and heteropolysaccharides.
Homopolysaccharides are composed of a single type of monosaccharide unit,
while heteropolysaccharides are composed of more than one type of
monosaccharide unit.
Examples of homopolysaccharides include starch, glycogen, and cellulose.
Starch and glycogen are storage polysaccharides found in plants and animals,
respectively, while cellulose is a structural polysaccharide found in plant cell
walls.
Examples of heteropolysaccharides include chondroitin sulphate, which is
found in cartilage, and heparin, which is an anticoagulant found in the body.
Based on function, polysaccharides can be classified into two main categories:
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
structural and storage polysaccharides. Structural polysaccharides provide
support
and rigidity to cells and tissues, while storage polysaccharides are used to
store energy.
Examples of structural polysaccharides include cellulose, chitin, and
peptidoglycan.
Cellulose is found in plant cell walls, chitin is found in the exoskeletons of
insects and crustaceans, and peptidoglycan is found in bacterial cell walls.
Examples of storage polysaccharides include starch and glycogen, which store
energy in plants and animals, respectively.
Explain the elongation phase of DNA replication. Describe how the
leading and lagging strands are synthesized, including the role of RNA
primers and the formation of Okazaki fragments with neat labelled
diagram.
Elongation – 3 marks
Synthesis of leading and lagging strand + role of RNA primers – 5 marks
Diagram 2 marks
Elongation Once the DNA Polymerase has attached to the original, unzipped
two strands of DNA (i.e. the template strands), it is able to start synthesizing
the new DNA to match the templates. It is essential to note that DNA
polymerase can only extend the primer by adding free nucleotides to the 3’ end.
One of the templates is read in a 3’ to 5’ direction, meaning the new strand will
be formed in a 5’ to 3’ direction.
This newly formed strand is referred to as the Leading Strand. Along this
strand, DNA Primase only needs to synthesize an RNA primer once, at the
beginning, to initiate DNA Polymerase. This is because DNA Polymerase can
extend the new DNA strand by reading the template 3′ to 5′, synthesizing in a
Q3   b.   5′ to 3′ direction as noted above.   [10]
CO-2; SO-2; BL-2   However, the other template strand (the lagging strand) is antiparallel and is
therefore read in a 5’ to 3’ direction. Continuous DNA synthesis, as in the
leading strand, would need to be in the 3′ to 5′ direction, which is impossible as
we cannot add bases to the 5′ end. Instead, as the helix unwinds, RNA primers
are added to the newly exposed bases on the lagging strand and DNA synthesis
occurs in fragments, but still in the 5′ to 3′ direction as before. These fragments
are known as Okazaki fragments.
Classify organisms based on the type of nitrogenous excretion with
examples, and explain how autotrophs and heterotrophs obtain
energy, by providing one example of each
Classification based on Nitrogenous Excretion - 6 marks
autotrophs, heterotrophs,– 4 Marks
Q4
a.   1. Ammonotellic: Organisms that primarily excrete ammonia (NH3) as their   [10]
CO-2; SO-2; BL-2
primary nitrogenous waste product. Ammonia is highly soluble in water and
toxic to tissues. It is a very toxic substance to tissues and extremely soluble in
water. Only one nitrogen atom is removed with it. A lot of water is needed for
the excretion of ammonia; about 0.5 L of water is needed per 1 g of nitrogen
to maintain ammonia levels in the excretory fluid below the level in body
fluids to prevent toxicity.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Ammonotelic animals, like aquatic protozoans, crustaceans, and echinoderms,
excrete ammonia directly into the water. Examples: Fish: Many aquatic fish
excrete excess ammonia into the water as it is highly soluble and can be
eliminated through diffusion. Amphibians: Amphibians, like frogs, often
excrete ammonia, which is excreted through their moist skin and kidneys.
2. Ureotellic: Organisms that excrete urea (a less toxic nitrogenous
compound) as their primary nitrogenous waste product. Urea is less toxic and
requires less water for excretion. It requires 0.05 L of water to excrete 1 g of
nitrogen, approximately only 10% of that required in ammonotelic organisms.
Ureotelic animals, such as humans and amphibians, efficiently excrete urea in
less water. Examples Mammals: Most mammals, including humans, excrete
urea through the kidneys as it requires less water for excretion compared to
ammonia. Marine Birds: Birds excrete urea in a semi-solid form as a way to
conserve water in their saltwater environments.
3. Uricotellic: Organisms that excrete uric acid, a highly insoluble
nitrogenous compound, as their primary nitrogenous waste product. Uric acid
is highly insoluble in water, requiring very little water for excretion. Uric acid
is less toxic than ammonia or urea. It contains four nitrogen atoms, and only a
small amount of water (about 0.001 L per 1 g of nitrogen) is needed for its
excretion. It can be stored in body tissues without toxic effects and is the most
efficient nitrogenous waste removal method. Uricotelic animals typically have
white pasty excreta, and some mammals, including humans, excrete uric acid
as a component of their urine in small amounts. Examples: Reptiles
(excluding birds): Many reptiles, such as snakes and lizards, excrete uric acid,
allowing them to conserve water in arid environments. Birds: While birds are
ureotellic, they further convert urea into uric acid to minimize water loss,
particularly in their concentrated urine and solid excreta
based on energy and carbon utilization
Autotrophs (Auto): Organisms capable of synthesizing their own organic
molecules (e.g., sugars) from inorganic sources such as carbon dioxide. Some
autotrophs, like green plants and algae, are phototrophs, which means they
convert electromagnetic energy from sunlight into chemical energy.
Autotrophs use electron transport chains or proton pumping to establish an
electrochemical gradient. While most autotrophs use water as the reducing
agent, some can use other hydrogen compounds, like hydrogen sulfide. They
act as producers in a food chain, providing energy for other organisms (in
contrast to heterotrophs that consume autotrophs). Autotrophs do not need a
living source of energy or organic carbon.
Examples: Plants: They use photosynthesis to convert carbon dioxide into
glucose, utilizing energy from sunlight. Cyanobacteria (Blue-Green Algae):
These photosynthetic bacteria produce their own organic compounds,
contributing to oxygen production in aquatic ecosystems.
Heterotrophs (Hetero): Organisms that rely on external sources for organic
molecules (e.g., obtaining carbon from consuming other organisms).
Heterotrophs are primary, secondary, and tertiary consumers in food chains
but not producers. More than 95% of living organisms are heterotrophic,
including animals, fungi, some bacteria, and protists. Chemoheterotrophs
(e.g., humans and mushrooms) use chemical energy, while photoheterotrophs
(e.g., green non-sulfur bacteria) use light for energy.
Examples: Animals: They consume plants or other animals to obtain organic
molecules for energy and growth. Fungi: Fungi obtain organic matter by
secreting enzymes to break down complex substances and then absorbing the
simpler nutrients.
Q4   Define the electron transport chain and oxidative phosphorylation. State   [10]
CO-3; SO-2; BL-1   b. the role of ATP as the energy currency, the function of high-energy electron
********** 7 **********

carriers and list the final products formed during the process, with neat

Definition of Electron Transport Chain (ETC) – 2 marks
Definition of Oxidative Phosphorylation   - 2 Marks
Role of ATP as energy currency   - 1 Mark
Role of NADH as high-energy electron carrier - 1 Mark
Role of FADH2 as high-energy electron carrier - 1 Mark
Final products formed - 1 Mark
Diagram – 2 Marks
Electron Transport Chain -The electron transport chain (ETC) is a series of
protein complexes and enzymes located in the inner mitochondrial membrane.
It transfers high-energy electrons from NADH and FADH2 through a chain of
redox reactions. This electron flow creates a proton gradient across the
membrane.
Oxidative Phosphorylation : Oxidative phosphorylation is the process of ATP
production using the energy stored in the proton gradient generated by the
ETC. ATP synthase uses this energy to convert ADP and inorganic phosphate
(Pi) into ATP.
Role of ATP: ATP serves as the cell’s energy currency, providing energy for
various cellular activities.
Role of NADH - NADH donates high-energy electrons to the ETC, driving
proton pumping and ATP synthesis.
Role of FADH2 - FADH2 also donates electrons to the ETC but enters at a
later complex than NADH.
Final Products (1 mark)- ATP: ~34 ATP per glucose molecule- Oxidized
carriers: NAD⁺ and FAD (recycled for earlier steps) water (H2O) being the
end product as oxygen (O2) acts as the final electron acceptor (1 mark)
(2 Marks)

Explain enzyme inhibition and describe its main types with neat
diagrammatic sketch. Give one example for each type.
Definition of enzyme inhibition+ types (2 marks)
Reversible Inhibition (6 marks)
competitive inhibition with one example (2 marks)
non-competitive inhibition with one example (2 marks)
uncompetitive inhibition with one example (2 marks)
Irreversible Inhibition with one example (2 marks)
Enzyme inhibition Enzyme inhibition is the process where molecules
(inhibitors) bind to enzymes and decrease their catalytic activity, regulating
Q5   metabolic pathways. It falls into two categories: reversible and irreversible,
b. each with unique subtypes. (2 marks)   [10]
CO-3; SO-2; BL-2   A. Reversible Inhibition:
1. Competitive Inhibition: \Competitive inhibitors are molecules that closely
resemble the substrate and compete for binding to the enzyme's active site.
Example: Statins, which compete with cholesterol for an enzyme's attention,
slowing down cholesterol production.
2. Non-Competitive Inhibition: Non-competitive inhibitors bind to the enzyme
at a location other than the active site, causing a conformational change that
reduces the enzyme's activity.
Example: Heavy metals like lead sticking to enzymes, making them work
slower.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
3. Uncompetitive Inhibition Uncompetitive inhibitors only bind to the
enzyme-substrate complex, preventing the release of the product.
Example: Methotrexate trapping an enzyme-substrate duo in a never-ending
ride, stopping the fun.
B. Irreversible Inhibition:
4. Covalent Modification: - Irreversible inhibition is a profound concept in
enzymology, characterized by the formation of a permanent and often
covalent bond between an inhibitor molecule and the enzyme. Unlike
reversible inhibition, irreversible inhibition leads to a lasting loss of enzyme
activity and is typically non competitive in nature.
In irreversible inhibition, the inhibitor molecule forms a covalent bond with
specific amino acid residues within the enzyme's active site or at other critical
locations. This covalent linkage results in permanent enzyme inactivation.
Example: Cyanide "gluing" an enzyme in our cells' energy factory, causing a
shutdown.

Explain the development of a nanoparticle-based vaccine delivery system
for cancer immunotherapy.
Development of a nanoparticle-based vaccine -1 mark
Types of nanoparticles- 1 Mark
Encapsulation of antigens and adjuvants – 2 Marks
Targeting strategy – 2 marks
Evaluation/Outcomes: 2 marks
Advantages/Significance: 2 Marks
Nanoparticle-based delivery systems have emerged as a promising strategy to
enhance vaccine efficacy by protecting antigens, targeting immune cells, and
improving controlled release.
Q6   Type of nanoparticles: Biodegradable polymeric nanoparticles, e.g., PLGA
b. (poly(lactic-co-glycolic acid)), used for safety and antigen/adjuvant   [10]
CO-2; SO-2 BL-2   encapsulation.
Encapsulation of antigens and adjuvants: Tumor-associated antigens (TAAs)
and immunostimulatory molecules (e.g., CpG oligonucleotides) are
encapsulated to protect from degradation and allow sustained release.
Targeting strategy: Surface modification (e.g., mannose) to target dendritic
cells, enhancing uptake and antigen presentation.
Evaluation/Outcomes: In vitro: efficient uptake by dendritic cells and
activation of T cells. In vivo: enhanced anti-tumor immunity, reduced tumor
growth, increased survival- .
Advantages/Significance: Stronger immune response, reduced systemic
toxicity, potential for personalized vaccines using patient-specific antigens.
What is biomimicry by process? Cite termite-mound inspired buildings as
an example to evidence it.
biomimicry by process – 2.5 Marks
Example - termite-mound inspired buildings – 2.5 Marks
biomimicry by process
This type of biomimicry involves imitating natural processes and
systems, such as photosynthesis or the water cycle. By mimicking these
Q7   processes, engineers and designers can develop new technologies and systems   [05]
a.
CO-1; SO-1 BL-1   that are more sustainable, efficient, and resilient.
Example:
The Eastgate Centre in Harare, Zimbabwe, is a building that uses biomimicry
of processes to regulate its temperature. The building was designed to mimic
the ventilation system of termite mounds, which use a system of vents and
tunnels to regulate their temperature. The Eastgate Centre uses a similar
system, with vents that open and close to regulate the flow of air, reducing the
need for air conditioning and heating.

Q7   (i)   Steroids: Steroids are a class of complex lipids that play   [10]
b.
CO-3; SO-2; BL-2   important roles in the body, including regulating metabolism,
immune function, and reproductive processes. They have a unique
structure consisting of four fused rings and vary in the functional
groups
attached to the rings. Examples of steroids include cholesterol,
which is important for cell membrane structure and function, and
hormone steroids such as testosterone and estrogen, which play
roles in sexual development and reproductive function. (2 Marks)
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
(ii)   Structural Proteins: Structural Proteins: Many proteins serve as
building materials of cells and tissues. They take part in the
formation of colloidal complex of protoplast, cell membranes,
organelles, extracellular matrices, and fibres. Some proteins form
supporting structures, e.g., elastin of ligaments, collagen of tendons,
cartilages, bone, and connective tissue. Keratin is the major
constituent of external protective structures of animals like hair,
feathers, horny layers of skin, nails, claws, etc (2 Marks)
(iii)   Saturated triglycerides: Saturated triglycerides have only saturated
fatty acids, which are solid at room temperature and are typically
found in animal products such as meat and dairy. (2 Marks)
(iv)   Receptors Proteins: A number of proteins present on the external
surface of the cell membrane act as receptor molecules. A receptor
protein molecule receives chemical signals from outside a cell.
When such chemical signals bind to a receptor, they cause some
form of cellular/tissue response. For example, when insulin is
released in the blood, the receptor proteins present on cell
membranes bind with this insulin. This binding changes the
permeability of the cell membrane, and glucose can enter the cell
through glucose channels.
(v)   Transport Proteins : Some proteins act as carriers that bind and
transport specific molecules across a membrane or in a body fluid.
Haemoglobin of RBCs transports oxygen in the body. RBCs contain
haemoglobin (Hb), which is made up of 4 polypeptide chains and
contains an iron ion. Iron has a strong affinity for oxygen

a useful model organism in medical science
contribution of microbes& Model organism- 2 Marks
Saccharomyces cerevisiae as Model in medical Science – 3 Marks
Microbes and model organisms play a pivotal role in drug discovery and
pharmaceutical research. Microbes like bacteria and fungi are valuable sources
of natural compounds that serve as the basis for many pharmaceutical drugs,
including antibiotics. These organisms produce a diverse range of secondary
metabolites with therapeutic potential. Model organisms, on the other hand,
provide crucial insights into the molecular and cellular processes underlying
various diseases. They help scientists understand disease mechanisms and test
potential drug candidates.
Q7   Saccharomyces cerevisiae (Yeast):   [05]
CO-2; SO-2; BL-2   c.   S. cerevisiae is a valuable model organism in medical science, particularly in
the study of aging and cellular processes, due to specific aspects:
Genetic Simplicity: The yeast's simple genetics make it a valuable tool for
exploring genes and pathways associated with aging and senescence.
Fermentation: Its pivotal role in fermentation makes   it relevant for
understanding metabolic processes that have implications for age-related
diseases and cellular function.
Applications: Yeast research is instrumental in understanding the genetic basis
of aging and age-related
diseases. It is also employed in studying cellular processes such as DNA
replication, gene regulation,
and signal transduction pathways with direct relevance to medical science.
Diseases Explored: While not directly related to medical diseases, yeast models
are used to study the fundamental mechanisms underlying age-related disorders,
DNA replication, and genetic pathways with implications for conditions like
cancer.
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
FRR IC 7 I OH
````
